**Prices come from a cost model, not from matching a competitor.** The drivers, in order of weight: Aquiline tracking conversion (~$0.10/shipment — **65% of unit cost at the 10,000-listing tier**), Keepa refresh tokens (~$0.010–0.015 per active product per month), Stripe fees, listing churn (negligible: 50% monthly churn on 1,000 listings costs ~$0.08, because a create is 8 tokens once while a refresh is 8 tokens 60× a month), and infrastructure. Every tier is verified profitable from 5 users onward AND for a single user who fills their quota (worst-case margin 18%; normal-usage margin 59–75%).

**The AO quota matches Easync's flat 10% of listings at every tier but one, and pricing lands 4–5% below theirs.** Easync uses Aquiline for tracking conversion as well, so the unit economics are comparable rather than structurally different — an earlier revision of this file claimed they did it in-house at zero marginal cost and used that to justify a much lower quota; that was wrong.

**Pro (10,000 listings) is the single exception at 7%.** Easync's own curve is a flat ~$22 per 1,000 listings at every tier except 10,000, which they price at ~$18.60 per 1,000 — 16% below their own curve, an anchor on the most-compared tier. Following that anchor down on both price *and* quota puts a fully-utilised user above what they pay, so the quota is trimmed there instead. Overage is an add-on sale (~$20 per 100 extra AO against a ~$10 cost), not a loss.

**Do not re-derive these numbers from a competitor's page without re-running the cost model** — the two are not interchangeable, and a competitor whose cost structure differs (Easync in-house tracking, AslDrop self-scraped pricing data instead of a Keepa licence) can sustain prices we cannot.

Plan prices, limits, active state, ordering, and Stripe price IDs are stored in billing catalog tables, not TypeScript constants or UI copy. Landing pricing reads the public catalog. Do not create a second plan model.

### Billing implementation

- Migrations `052_create_billing_foundation.sql` and `053_billing_quota_enforcement.sql` create the catalog, subscriptions, usage periods, listing/AO reservations, and webhook inbox.
- Backend module: `apps/api/src/modules/billing/`; frontend feature: `apps/web/src/features/billing/`.
- **Billing is a first-class routed page, not a Settings drawer (2026-08-12).** `BillingPage` renders at `/:locale/billing`, with its own seller-sidebar entry near Settings (`AppLayout`) and its own `routeMeta.ts` breadcrumb entry — under `AppLayout` only, same as every other seller page. It was originally a `BillingDrawer` opened from the Settings hub (`?drawer=billing`); that drawer is deleted. `/:locale/settings/billing` and `/:locale/settings?drawer=billing` both redirect to `/:locale/billing` (the latter via a small `SettingsRoute` wrapper in `App.tsx`, since a query param can't be matched by a second `<Route>` on the same path) so old links keep working. The operator Admin Billing tab (`/admin` → Billing) is unrelated and unchanged.
- **Stripe is the ONLY payment provider (Billing/Invoicing/Tax), as of 2026-08-16.** `StripeBillingProvider` (`apps/api/src/modules/billing/billing-provider.ts`) is the single implementation of `BillingProviderPort`. There is no provider-selection concept, no `BILLING_PROVIDER` switch, and no stand-in "local provider" class — **Stripe's own test mode is what local dev and the test environment run against**, so a fake provider would never be exercised. When `STRIPE_SECRET_KEY` is absent the same class is still injected; it reports `isConfigured() === false` and throws `billing.errors.providerNotConfigured` (→ 409) on checkout/portal, while catalog + summary keep working. The Stripe client is built lazily because `new Stripe('')` throws, so an unconfigured deployment must still boot.
  - **A Paddle implementation lived here until 2026-08-16 and was deleted** (provider, event applier, HMAC verification, `POST /billing/webhooks`, all `PADDLE_*` env). No Paddle account ever existed and no Paddle checkout ever ran, so no DB row references it. It was removed rather than left as unreachable dead code; recover it from git if the business ever revisits Paddle.
  - **`BillingProvider.LOCAL` still exists and is NOT a provider.** It is the state of a `billing_customers` row that has never reached checkout — every user starts `local` (`ensureLocalCustomer`/`startTrialOnce`) and flips to `stripe` on first checkout. It is also what the catalog/summary DTOs report when no key is configured, so the FE can hide the checkout button instead of surfacing a 409 after the click. Do not remove the enum member: existing rows carry it.
  - **Checkout and portal are both Stripe-hosted, redirect-based.** `POST /billing/checkout` → `createCheckout` makes a Checkout Session (`mode: 'subscription'`, `automatic_tax: { enabled: true }`, **never** `payment_method_types` — omitting it enables dynamic payment methods) and returns its URL; the FE redirects. `GET /billing/portal` → `stripe.billingPortal.sessions.create`. Card data, SCA/3DS and PCI scope stay entirely with Stripe — nothing payment-sensitive runs in this codebase. `apps/web/src/features/billing/` needed no changes: it only ever branched on `enforcementEnabled` and the generic 409, never on the provider string.
  - **The Stripe customer is created and linked at checkout-creation time, not from a webhook — this is load-bearing, not an optimisation.** `ensureStripeCustomer` creates the customer, `linkProviderCustomer` writes `provider`/`provider_customer_id` onto the local row, and only then is the session opened. Webhook delivery order is not guaranteed: linking on `checkout.session.completed` (the obvious first design, and what shipped for a few hours) meant a `customer.subscription.created` arriving first found no local customer and was dropped as `no_customer` — **the user had paid and got no subscription row**, with no retry until some unrelated later event fired. Linking first makes the customer always resolvable before any webhook can reference it, and removes the need to handle `checkout.session.completed` at all.
    - This also fixed a pre-existing latent bug in the same plumbing: nothing had *ever* updated `billing_customers.provider` off `'local'` after checkout, so `findCustomerByProviderId` could never match for any provider.
  - **Webhook: one endpoint, `POST /billing/webhooks/stripe`.** Verified with the Stripe SDK's own `stripe.webhooks.constructEvent` (never a hand-rolled HMAC) against `req.rawBody`, which `main.ts` already captures globally via `rawBody: true` — no middleware to add. That verification is the entire security boundary, so it runs before the body is trusted for anything; a missing/invalid signature or missing secret is a 401 (fail-closed). `BillingWebhookProcessor.process` runs the insert→claim→stale-check→apply→mark pipeline (idempotent on `provider_event_id`, safe under concurrent redelivery).
  - **Only `customer.subscription.created|updated|deleted` mutate state**, and `stripe-event-applier.ts` (pure + tested) reads the subscription object's own `status` field rather than inferring from the event type — Stripe's status is authoritative regardless of which fired. `invoice.paid`/`invoice.payment_failed` are recorded in the inbox but deliberately not applied: the transitions they imply (e.g. `past_due`) already arrive via `customer.subscription.updated`.
  - **`customer_update: { address: 'auto', name: 'auto' }` is REQUIRED on the session, and it is required *because* the customer is created up-front.** `automatic_tax` needs an address to compute against; a Stripe Customer that exists with no address makes Checkout **reject the session outright** rather than collect one ("Automatic tax calculation in Checkout requires a valid address on the Customer"). So the two decisions are coupled: linking the customer early (the webhook-ordering fix above) is what creates the addressless customer, and `customer_update` is what lets Checkout write the collected address back onto it. Every checkout failed with a 500 until this was added. `billing_address_collection: 'required'` is set alongside it, and `tax_id_collection: { enabled: true }` — sellers are businesses, and in the EU the VAT-ID field is what triggers reverse charge, so omitting it charges VAT to buyers who should not pay it.
  - **Billing failure keys are mapped to HTTP statuses in `billing.controller.ts` (`BILLING_ERROR_STATUS` + `rethrowBillingError`), and the seller-facing strings live in `billing.json` under `billing.errors.*`.** The provider and service layers throw plain `Error`s carrying an i18n key; with no mapping the global filter turned all of them into a 500 whose body reads only "Internal server error", so a configuration problem reached the seller as the same blank "an error occurred" dialog as a real crash — the actual cause visible only in `error-*.log`. This is the same audience split as `ListingFailureCode`: the seller gets a localized reason, the raw provider text stays in the log. A key with no mapping stays a 500 (a genuine defect), and a key with no i18n entry renders raw — add both halves together.
  - **Stripe Tax**: `automatic_tax` on the Checkout Session carries onto the Subscription it creates, so renewals are taxed with no per-renewal code. **It silently collects ZERO tax — no error — until a head-office address AND at least one active registration exist in the Dashboard** (Tax → Settings). Registration is a legal decision for the account owner and their tax advisor; never infer or automate it. Products are created with tax code `txcd_10103001` (SaaS), not the too-broad default `txcd_10000000`. Note Stripe is **not** a Merchant of Record: SellerHill remains liable for registration, remittance and filing.
  - **Catalog mirroring**: `billing_plans.provider_product_id`/`billing_plan_prices.provider_price_id` must hold real Stripe ids before checkout works — run `pnpm --filter api stripe:sync-catalog` (`apps/api/src/scripts/stripe-sync-catalog.ts`; direct `pg` Pool, no NestJS bootstrap, same convention as `user-set-role.ts`). Idempotent: a row that already has an id is skipped, since Stripe Prices are immutable once created (which matches the existing effective-dated pricing model — a price change is a new row, not an edit).
- **The free trial is rationed per eBay STORE, not per email (migration `084`).** Every data screen sits behind `EbayAccountGuard`, so a trial without a connected store is worthless — which makes the eBay store, not the email address, the scarce resource worth farming. `ebay_accounts` already had a UNIQUE `(seller_id, marketplace_id)` and there is no disconnect endpoint, so two live accounts can never hold one store; the hole was the *sequential* one, since `ebay_accounts.user_id` is `ON DELETE CASCADE` — delete the account and the store is free to be reconnected under a fresh registration for another trial. `ebay_trial_ledger` is deliberately **not** FK'd to `ebay_accounts` so it survives that deletion (`first_user_id` is `ON DELETE SET NULL`); its `(seller_id, marketplace_id)` primary key is the enforcement, via `INSERT … ON CONFLICT DO NOTHING` in `claimEbayTrial`. `BillingService.assertEbayStoreMayConnect` runs in the eBay OAuth callback **before** the account row is written, and **a paying customer is never blocked** — the rule refuses a second free ride, not a returning customer. It **fails open**: a billing outage allows the connect, because the ledger is a fraud control and failing closed would lock out paying customers whenever billing is degraded.
- **Trial size is 50 listings / 20 tracking conversions / 20 AO, and it runs 30 days** (listings tightened from 1,000 by `083`; the conversion meter took over the trial's old 10 in `085` and doubled to 20 in `097`; AO stays at 20). At 1,000 listings it was larger than the Micro plan it exists to convert into, so there was no reason to upgrade before it expired. **Duration is the `billing.trialDays` platform setting** — DB override → `BILLING_TRIAL_DAYS` env → code default, which is **30** as of `097` (was `7`); registry bounds 1–90. Costs **~$1.00–1.25 per trial user** in Keepa + Aquiline (≈$110–150 for 100 trial users; hard ceiling ~$345 if every trial user fills both the 20-conversion and 20-AO meters).
  - Env: `STRIPE_SECRET_KEY`, `STRIPE_WEBHOOK_SECRET` (both optional; absent = fail-safe 409). See `apps/api/.env.example`. `VITE_STRIPE_PUBLISHABLE_KEY` (`apps/web/.env.example`, `Dockerfile.web`, both Coolify compose files) is provisioned but **unconsumed by any FE code** — the redirect flow needs no Stripe.js/Elements surface; it is ready the moment one is built.
- **`BILLING_ENFORCEMENT_ENABLED` defaults to `true` as of `097`** (it was `false` throughout the pre-launch transition). This is the master switch for everything in this section: with it `false`, every quota gate — the 30-day trial's own limits included — is a no-op, so the trial has no cost ceiling at all. Flipping it on is part of the same change that made the trial a real 30-day/20-conversion offer, because a limit nobody enforces is not a limit. A fresh install with no DB override and no env var now enforces quotas; set it `false` per deployment only to stage a slower rollout. **It is a registered `platform_settings` key (`billing.enforcementEnabled`) and is admin-panel-editable** (`/admin` → Settings → Billing), resolving DB override → env → default via `PlatformSettingsService.getBoolean`, same as every other panel-tunable knob. `QuotaEnforcementService.isEnabled()` always read it correctly this way; `BillingService.getCatalog()`/`getSummary()` did not — they called `resolveBillingConfig()`, a pure env-only reader with no DB access, so the summary's `transition='full_access'` copy stayed stuck on the env default even after an operator toggled it in the panel (fixed 2026-08-15: both methods now resolve `enforcementEnabled` via `platformSettings.getBoolean()` directly, in parallel with their other repository reads; `getConfig()`/`resolveBillingConfig()` remain env-only and are still correct for the Stripe key fields, which are NOT DB-overridable).
- Stripe webhooks use raw-body signature verification (via the Stripe SDK), idempotent inbox processing, stale-event protection, and subscription state updates. JWT must never be treated as the billing source of truth.

### Entitlement & quota enforcement (rewritten 2026-08-21)

**Everything this section used to claim was documented before it was implemented.** The quota gate never read `subscription.status`; `billing_usage_periods` had no INSERT anywhere, so monthly meters never reset; nothing released a listing slot when a listing ended; and the manual Amazon-link path had no quota at all. None of it failed loudly because `BILLING_ENFORCEMENT_ENABLED` has never been switched on. `entitlement-enforcement.guard.spec.ts` now source-greps every one of those calls so the gap cannot silently reopen.

**Entitlement is one function.** `resolveEntitlementState(status)` (`packages/shared/src/domain/billing/entitlement.ts`, pure + tested) maps `ACTIVE`/`TRIALING` -> `ACTIVE`, `PAST_DUE`/`CANCELED`/`ENDED` -> `SUSPENDED`, and `null` -> `NONE`. `NONE` is **not** suspension: it is the pre-billing state every existing account is in, and callers fail OPEN on it — collapsing the two would lock out every user the moment enforcement is turned on. Five enforcement points read this one function instead of re-deriving the status list, for the same reason `fulfillment-state-sql.guard.spec.ts` exists.

**Suspension is immediate on the first failed charge** (operator decision, 2026-08-21 — a grace window was offered and declined). `PAST_DUE` therefore suspends. Nothing needs an "un-suspend" action: every gate is status-driven, so the next `customer.subscription.updated` reporting `active` reopens all of them on its own.

**Four things stop when an account is suspended**, and the last two are what was costing real money:
- the quota gate — `resolveSubscriptionContext` returns `limitValue: 0`, so every existing caller refuses through the path it already had;
- auto-fulfill, with its own `AutoFulfillBlockedReason.SUBSCRIPTION_SUSPENDED` — never folded into `QUOTA_EXHAUSTED`, because "pay the invoice" and "upgrade the plan" send the seller to different screens;
- **the Keepa refresh claim**, which had zero billing awareness: a lapsed 5,000-listing account burned ~$55/month of tokens indefinitely. `products` is a shared cache, so the rule is "at least one ACTIVE listing belongs to an entitled owner" — the non-payer's listing goes stale, a paying seller on the same ASIN is untouched. The billing join is built conditionally, so with enforcement off the statement is byte-identical to the original;
- **Aquiline conversion**, which falls back to the local pass-through.

**Three metered dimensions, three different measures — this distinction is load-bearing:**

| Dimension | Measure | Counted as | Exhaustion |
|---|---|---|---|
| `listings_per_month` | **level** | `COUNT(ACTIVE listings)` + in-flight reservations. No period. | blocks new listings |
| `amazon_orders_per_month` | monthly **flow** | reservations scoped to the open usage period | blocks new orders |
| `tracking_conversions_per_month` | monthly **flow** | `COUNT(orders converted this UTC month)` — no ledger | **degrades, never blocks** |

- **Listings are a level, so ending one frees a slot.** A successful create/publish now RELEASES its reservation — the ACTIVE listing row is the durable entitlement, and the reservation only covers the minutes a bulk create is in flight. Previously "consume" was a no-op that left the row `reserved` forever, which made the quota a ratchet: the count could only climb, and deleting a listing freed nothing.
- **`BillingRepositoryService.reserveSlot` builds its INSERT column list PER TABLE** (`billing_listing_reservations` has `listing_id` and no `ebay_order_id`; `billing_ao_reservations` is the mirror). A single column list naming both fails on whichever table lacks one with `column "…" does not exist`. This broke **every** single-slot reserve — `reserveForPublish` (draft publish) and `reserveForAmazonOrder` — whenever `BILLING_ENFORCEMENT_ENABLED` was on; only `reserveSlotsBulk` (create path) worked, because it has its own correct INSERT. The failure surfaced as a generic "could not publish, check stock and eBay account" modal (fixed 2026-09-08). Mirror `reserveSlotsBulk` if you touch either statement.
- **`billing_usage_periods` is opened lazily on read** (`ensureOpenUsagePeriod`, guarded by the table's own unique constraint) — the repo's `ensureSeeded` / `resolveProductData` idiom, not a cron. A period that cannot be opened yields a used-count of **0**, never the unscoped count: bookkeeping trouble must not read as quota exhaustion.
- **Conversions deliberately have no reservation table.** A conversion is one synchronous act with a durable record (`orders.tracking_converted_at`), so the count is read from the orders themselves and cannot drift. A failed conversion writes nothing and therefore costs no quota, which is also the correct billing answer since the provider is only paid on success.
- **Exhausting the conversion quota does NOT stop the shipment.** eBay still gets a real, scannable number — the raw Amazon one. The seller keeps selling; only the supplier concealment stops. Logged at `error`, because nothing else in the flow fails and the seller would otherwise never learn.

**The manual link path is metered like the automatic one.** `POST /amazon/orders/:orderId/link-amazon` reserves an AO slot before the scrape — `BillingLimitKey.AMAZON_ORDERS_PER_MONTH`'s own docstring always said the quota covers "auto-fulfill + manual link". Without it a seller at their limit could place the order by hand and link it: identical cost to us, zero quota consumed. Every failure path releases the reservation, because a slot held for a link that never happened is one the seller can never recover.

**Quota usage is computed live, never read from `billing_usage_periods.used_qty`.** That column exists and nothing has ever incremented it, so every consumer — including the Action Center's quota warnings — reported zero for every seller and could not fire. `BillingSummaryDto.quotas` is derived at read time from the same sources the gate refuses on, so the warning and the refusal cannot disagree.

**Monthly quota windows follow the Stripe billing period, not the calendar month.** Stripe bills on the subscription anniversary, so a UTC-calendar-month meter handed a seller who subscribed mid-month roughly two allowances per payment. `resolveQuotaWindow` (`quota-helpers.ts`, pure + tested) is the single resolver: it returns the subscription's own `currentPeriodStart`/`currentPeriodEnd` as the window, falling back to `utcMonthBounds` only when there is no subscription or the row is malformed (a bad row must never yield an unbounded window). **`periodStart` never moves without a confirmed payment** — usage is counted from it, so pinning it makes a fresh allowance arithmetically impossible; an earlier design that rolled the start forward by whole months on a stale window really did reset the quota on no evidence of payment and was rejected. A **6-hour grace** (`billing.webhookGraceHours` → `BILLING_WEBHOOK_GRACE_HOURS` → `DEFAULT_WEBHOOK_GRACE_HOURS = 6`) extends only `periodEnd`: without it every paying seller is briefly suspended at their own renewal instant, long enough for a shipped order to be held. A window still stale past that grace resolves to `QuotaWindowOutcome.UNPAID`, and `resolveEffectiveEntitlement` maps that to `SUSPENDED` **at read time** — webhook silence is not evidence the account is paid up. Nothing persists that inferred suspension, so the next real `customer.subscription.updated` clears it by itself; the operator recovery when a webhook is genuinely lost is `billing:reconcile` (below).

- Quota exhaustion uses shared enums/reasons and must not be represented by hardcoded status strings. Downgrades never disable existing listings; they block new create/publish operations only.

### The priced meter is tracking conversions, not automatic orders (2026-08-21)

Migration `085`. The per-unit cash cost of an order is the Aquiline conversion (~$0.10/shipment), not the order — and once a seller can convert only Amazon Logistics numbers (see "Tracking conversion" below), the order count stops predicting our cost entirely: 300 orders with 100 TB* numbers costs the same as 100 orders. So `tracking_conversions_per_month` took over the numbers `amazon_orders_per_month` used to carry, and AO became a ceiling at **2x the conversion quota** — on the paid tiers. Migration `097` doubled the trial's conversion quota to 20 without touching its AO, so the trial alone runs 20/20 (1x): its cost ceiling is the 20 conversions either way, and a trial user hitting an AO wall below that is the opposite of the point.

**Prices did not change**, and the 12-tier cost model is unaffected: Aquiline exposure is still capped at exactly the figures it was built on.

**AO is an anti-abuse ceiling, not a capacity guarantee — do not read it as one.** Orders are the opposite of free on the resource that actually runs out. Every order consumes the platform-wide Playwright pool (`AMAZON_GLOBAL_CONCURRENCY`, 5 concurrent => ~13M browser-seconds/month, of which the `amazon-order-sync` tick already takes ~4.2M), and an **unconverted order costs MORE of it than a converted one** (~450s vs ~330s): a converted shipment stops Amazon polling because delivery arrives by provider webhook, while an unconverted one is scraped until delivered. That inverts the incentive — "convert only TB*" saves the seller quota and costs us browser time. Platform throughput is ~20,000-26,000 orders/month, i.e. ~40-50 per user per month at 500 users, so Growth's 600 is roughly 12x the sustainable average. Protection comes from monitoring; the lever is `AMAZON_GLOBAL_CONCURRENCY` (5 -> 15 is about +3-4.5 GB RAM).

| Plan | Listings | **Tracking conversions** | Automatic orders |
|---|---:|---:|---:|
| Trial | 50 | 20 | 20 |
| Lite | 200 | 25 | 50 |
| Nano | 500 | 50 | 100 |
| Micro | 1,000 | 100 | 200 |
| Starter | 2,000 | 150 | 300 |
| Basic | 3,000 | 200 | 400 |
| Plus | 4,000 | 250 | 500 |
| Growth | 5,000 | 300 | 600 |
| Advanced | 7,500 | 350 | 700 |
| Pro | 10,000 | 500 | 1,000 |
| Elite | 15,000 | 600 | 1,200 |
| Business | 20,000 | 700 | 1,400 |
| Enterprise | 25,000 | 800 | 1,600 |

Migration `085` derives AO from the conversion row (`= conversions x 2`) rather than multiplying in place — an in-place multiply is **not idempotent**, and a replayed run would silently produce a 4x ceiling with nothing to show it had happened.

### Choosing a plan means two different things (2026-08-21)

**Stripe does NOT refuse a second subscription for a customer who already has one**, so "choose this plan" reaching checkout while subscribed creates a second subscription and bills for both. Nothing guarded against this. The two paths are now distinct and the UI says which one it is:

- **No Stripe subscription** (a local trial, or nothing) → `POST /billing/checkout`, a real subscribe. The trial lives only in our tables and has no Stripe subscription to reprice, so this is correct for a trialling seller.
- **A live Stripe subscription** → `POST /billing/change-plan`, billed by DIRECTION rather than one fixed proration mode. `resolvePlanChangeDirection` (pure, shared) decides upgrade vs. downgrade from the catalog price at the same interval on both sides; `changePlan` and `previewPlanChange` call it with the identical argument order so the previewed figure can never disagree with what actually gets charged. **Upgrade**: `stripe.subscriptions.update` with `proration_behavior: 'always_invoice'` + `payment_behavior: 'error_if_incomplete'` — the prorated difference is charged immediately, and a declined card fails the whole update, leaving the seller on the plan they already paid for. **Downgrade**: `provider.scheduleDowngrade` creates (or reuses) a Stripe Subscription Schedule — the current price runs to period end, the new price starts after, no money moves today. This reverses the `create_prorations` design from 2026-08-21: Stripe bills IN ADVANCE, so `create_prorations` handed an upgrading seller the higher quota the moment the update applied and billed the difference up to 30 days later — a Lite seller ($19.99) could jump to Enterprise, consume 25,000 listings and 800 tracking conversions (~$80 of Aquiline cost alone), and let the card fail before the invoice ever arrived. An upgrade first releases any pending downgrade schedule (`cancelScheduledChange`, best-effort — a subscription with no schedule is the normal case and already no-ops there) so a stale schedule can't fire a month later and undo a change the seller just paid for.
- `BillingSummaryDto.hasProviderSubscription` is what tells the FE which applies; the plan card's label follows it ("Choose Growth" vs "Switch to Growth"), because a button whose text does not match what it does is how the wrong one gets clicked.
- **`metadata.plan_id` moves with the price on every change.** The webhook applier resolves the local plan from subscription metadata, so repricing without updating it would have the subscription report the OLD plan back on its next update.
- **The plan is written locally the moment Stripe accepts the reprice, not when the webhook lands** (`BillingService.changePlan` → `repository.updateSubscriptionPlan`). The FE invalidates and refetches its summary as soon as the mutation resolves, while `customer.subscription.updated` arrives a second or two later — so relying on the webhook alone showed the seller their OLD plan and OLD quotas immediately after a successful upgrade, with nothing scheduled to correct it until they reloaded by hand (observed live 2026-08-22). Safe to write ahead of the webhook because Stripe has already returned success for the exact price we asked for: the new plan IS the truth at that point, not a prediction. The webhook then re-applies the same `plan_id` (it reads `metadata.plan_id`, which we set in the same call), so this is a head start rather than a second source of truth. The local write is best-effort — a failure is reconciled by the webhook and must not turn a completed upgrade into an error the seller sees.
- **The prorated amount scales with time left in the period, not with the headline price gap — that part is unchanged; only WHEN it is charged moved (2026-08-23, see the direction split above).** Stripe credits the unused part of the old plan and charges the prorated new one: Nano→Micro ($24.99→$29.99) is ~$4.16 on day 5 of a 30-day cycle but ~$0.83 on day 25. An upgrade now charges that figure immediately (`always_invoice`) instead of waiting for the next invoice (`create_prorations`, the previous design) — the confirm dialog (see below) is what turns "an unexpected debit right after clicking upgrade" from a support-ticket risk into a disclosed, confirmed charge.
- **Switching plans deliberately does NOT redirect to Stripe, and Checkout could not do it anyway** — opening Checkout for an existing subscriber creates a SECOND subscription (see the top of this section). The Stripe-hosted alternative is the Billing Portal's own plan switcher (Dashboard → Settings → Billing → Customer portal → "Customers can switch plans"), which needs no code since "Manage billing" already opens the portal; it was considered and declined on 2026-08-22 to keep the seller in-app. **The prorated-figure gap this used to leave is closed (2026-08-23)**: `POST /billing/plan-change/preview` calls `stripe.invoices.createPreview` with the target price and returns the real amount, currency and effective date — Stripe's own arithmetic, including tax and any discount, not a client-side estimate — and the billing page shows it in a confirm dialog before `change-plan` is ever called. A downgrade preview short-circuits to `amountDueMicros: 0` before that Stripe call, because a downgrade moves no money today.
- "Manage billing" is unrelated and stays the Stripe Billing Portal: card, invoices, cancel. It is disabled without a Stripe subscription, since there is nothing for the portal to show.

### Subscription integrity: three layers, and a live billing page (2026-08-23)

**One layer was already in place when the 2026-08-22 incident happened — `hasProviderSubscription` was already the gate, and it still let two extra subscriptions through**, because it read OUR database and our database was wrong twice over: D1, the subscription row was never persisted at all (see below); D2, nothing ever closed the local trial row when a real subscription started, so `findCurrentSubscription` kept returning the trial row — `providerSubscriptionId: null` — ahead of the real one, and `hasProviderSubscription` read that as "not yet subscribed." A check against local state cannot be trusted once local state is itself what broke, so the fix is three independent layers, not one better check:

- **A1 — close the trial the moment it's paid.** `stripe-event-applier.ts`'s `customer.subscription.created` handler calls `repository.endTrialSubscriptionsForUser(customer.userId)` before upserting the subscription. Best-effort — a failure here must not reject the webhook, since Stripe would then redeliver an event we already half-applied — and the repository method is scoped to `provider_subscription_id IS NULL`, so it can never touch a paying customer's row.
- **A2 — a provider-backed row always outranks a local trial row.** `findCurrentSubscription`'s `ORDER BY` now sorts `(provider_subscription_id IS NULL)` ahead of the status `CASE` (which used to put `trialing` first on its own). A1 should make this unreachable; it exists because "should be unreachable" is exactly what D1 and D2 both were.
- **A3 — ask Stripe, not our tables.** `createCheckout` calls `provider.hasActiveProviderSubscription`, which lists the customer's Stripe subscriptions directly and treats `active`/`trialing`/`past_due`/`unpaid`/`paused` as all already-subscribed (`paused` counts because it is still a live Stripe object that can resume billing — excluding it would reopen the same hole from the other side). A hit refuses checkout with `billing.errors.alreadySubscribed` → 409, and the FE routes the seller to plan-change instead of retrying checkout. **This is the one layer that cannot be fooled by our own database being wrong**, which is exactly what A1 and A2 cannot promise on their own. A lookup failure fails CLOSED — it throws rather than being read as "no subscription," because a refused checkout is recoverable and a duplicate subscription is a refund.

**Customer creation is serialized under the same discipline.** `withUserBillingLock` (a Postgres advisory lock keyed on the user) wraps both `createCheckout` and `createAddonCheckout`, with the local customer row re-read INSIDE the lock before Stripe is ever asked to create one. Without it, two concurrent checkouts from a brand-new user could each reach `provider.ensureCustomer` and mint two separate Stripe customers; since `billing_customers.user_id` is UNIQUE the local row can only point at one, silently orphaning whichever paid subscription got linked second. `subscription-integrity.guard.spec.ts` source-greps A1 through A3 plus the lock ordering, the same way `entitlement-enforcement.guard.spec.ts` locks its own invariants — every defect above was in the "documented but not implemented" class, and that class recurs.

**Invoices, the saved card, and the next-charge amount are read LIVE from Stripe on every request — there is no local mirror.** `BillingService.getDetails`/`getInvoices` call `provider.getBillingDetails`/`listInvoices` directly; nothing about them is ever written to our tables or read back from them. A webhook-fed local copy was considered and rejected: two records of the same money is the failure mode that matters most on a billing page (Stripe refunds something, our copy keeps showing the old figure), and it would buy only offline availability of history nobody reads during an outage. The card shown is the customer's `invoice_settings.default_payment_method`, never the first attached one — several accumulate because each Checkout run attaches a new one, and showing the wrong one puts a false number on the one screen whose entire purpose is being right. Invoice amounts carry the currency Stripe actually charged (`mapStripeInvoice`, `stripe-invoice-mapper.ts`), uppercased — Adaptive Pricing genuinely bills a Turkish seller in TRY, so assuming USD would misreport what left their account — and an unpaid invoice reports `amount_due`, not `amount_paid` (which is 0 on an open invoice and would tell a suspended seller they owe nothing).

**`GET /billing/summary` deliberately makes no Stripe call, and must never gain one.** `AppLayout` calls it on every page load — it drives the suspended-account redirect — so provider latency there would slow the whole app, not just the billing page. `BillingService.getSummary` reads only `findCurrentSubscription`/`loadPlanWithPricing`/`findOpenUsagePeriods`, all local. The live-from-Stripe data got its own endpoints instead (`GET /billing/details`, `GET /billing/invoices`), called only by the billing page, so a Stripe outage degrades the billing page and nothing else in the app.

**Top-up checkouts set `invoice_creation: { enabled: true }`, or the purchase is invisible in "what did I pay for."** A `mode: 'payment'` Checkout Session creates NO invoice by default; without this flag on `createAddonCheckout`, a seller who bought a conversions pack would see no trace of it in invoice history even though the charge went through.

**`allow_promotion_codes` is on the subscription checkout only.** `createCheckout` sets it so Checkout renders its own "Add promotion code" field (coupons live in the Stripe Dashboard — no local coupon model). `createAddonCheckout` deliberately omits it: a discount on a consumable already priced against a hard ~$0.10/conversion supplier cost erodes a thin margin with no acquisition benefit — a coupon is for winning a subscriber, not for cheapening a top-up.

### The first real Stripe subscription (2026-08-22)

The first end-to-end sandbox subscribe ran on this date and surfaced two defects that no amount of unit testing would have caught, because both live in the seam between Stripe and us:

- **No subscription could EVER be persisted.** `billing_subscriptions.provider_subscription_id` carried a plain (non-unique) partial index from migration `052`, while `upsertSubscriptionByProvider` has always used `ON CONFLICT (provider_subscription_id) WHERE provider_subscription_id IS NOT NULL`. Postgres requires a real unique constraint/index as an ON CONFLICT target, so every `customer.subscription.created` webhook died with *"there is no unique or exclusion constraint matching the ON CONFLICT specification"* → 500 → the seller paid and got nothing. Fixed by migration `088`. The failure was invisible for the whole life of `052` because no real Stripe subscription had ever existed to trigger the insert.
- **Stripe's return URLs have no locale segment, and the router read the first segment as one.** `success_url` / `cancel_url` / the portal `return_url` are built as `${FRONTEND_URL}/billing?...` (`billing-provider.ts`) — the backend cannot know which locale the seller was on when checkout started. `App.tsx`'s locale-less redirect list covered `/register`, `/login`, `/dashboard`, … but **not** `/billing`, so `"billing"` matched the `:locale` param, the remaining path was empty, and that route's `index` redirect sent a paying customer to **`/billing/register`**. Fixed with a `/billing` → `LocaleRedirect` entry carrying `preserveQuery` (the `checkout=success&session_id=…` and `topup=…` params have to survive). **Any new backend-built URL pointing at a seller page needs the same treatment** — the backend has no locale to work with, so the router has to supply one.

**The billing page is ONE plan card, and the plans live in a DRAWER.**
- The subscription card and the usage card answered halves of one question ("what am I on and how much is left"), and the identity half was a vertical stack of four short strings with no hierarchy. Now: name + status badge on one line, one muted meta line beneath, then the quota block under a divider in the same card.
- **Twelve plan cards are not page content.** Below the fold they turned the billing screen into a price list whose actual subject — the current plan and what is left of it — was the small part at the top. They now open in a `Drawer` from the plan card's one control, which also hosts the Stripe portal link for sellers who have a subscription to manage.
- **That control is never disabled.** It used to be greyed out exactly when the seller had no subscription — the moment they most need to choose one — leaving a dead button beside a wall of cards.
- **The meta line is assembled in the container**, because what belongs on it depends on the kind of plan. A trial has no billing interval and does not renew; the old line read "Monthly billing · Next renewal: 20 Aug" for a free trial that had already **ended**. Labels are `trialEnds` / `trialEnded` / `nextRenewal` / `accessEnds` by status, and `TRIAL_PLAN_SLUG` (shared) replaced the bare `'trial'` literals that decided it.
- **The notice only renders for states the seller can act on.** `active` / `full_access` put an alert-shaped "your subscription is active" above the usage on a screen where nothing was wrong; the status badge already says it.
- **Quota reads as rings, one per row.** A two-column grid left the third meter orphaned and needed a breakpoint to survive a phone; a vertical list needs none. `ProgressRing` (`packages/ui`) mirrors `ProgressBar`'s variants so "over limit" looks the same whichever shape a screen uses.
- **Both figures come from `summary.quotas`, never half from the plan.** `quotas[].limitValue` is the EFFECTIVE ceiling (plan + credits) and is what the gate enforces. Reading the limit from the catalog plan showed a seller who had just topped up their old ceiling, and rendered "not included" for a dimension the plan row happened not to carry. The used figure is also no longer printed twice — it appeared alone AND inside "used of limit", so a full quota read "50 50 / 50".

### A refused create leaves nothing behind (2026-08-21)

**A create refused by billing used to leave a phantom job.** `reserveForBulkCreate` keys its reservations on the `listing_job_items` ids, so `createJob` has to write the rows before the gate can run — and the gate threw AFTER them. The seller got a red dialog *and* a job stuck in the import list at "0 / N, waiting" forever, because nothing was queued to advance it and nothing was queued to fail it either.

- `ListingsService.deleteUnstartedJob` rolls it back, and is deliberately narrow: `status = pending` **and** no item in a non-draft state, so it can never be repurposed into something that discards real work. Cleanup is best-effort and never masks the refusal — the seller must be told why they were refused even if the tidy-up fails.
- **The refusal is now a 409 with a reason, not a 500.** The quota gate throws plain `Error`s carrying a `name` on purpose (the queue worker classifies by it — `classifyListingFailure`), but nothing translated them at the HTTP boundary, so an expired trial reached the seller as "Internal server error" with the real cause only in `error-*.log`. `LISTING_REFUSAL_KEYS` in `listings.controller.ts` maps them; same audience split as `ListingFailureCode`.
- **Suspension and a full quota are separate errors.** A suspended account reports `limitValue: 0`, so the quota check refuses it too — but "50 in use (limit 0)" is nonsense text for somebody whose trial expired, and it points at the wrong fix (upgrade the plan rather than buy one). `SubscriptionSuspendedError` vs `QuotaExhaustedError`; both still classify to `QUOTA_EXHAUSTED` on a job item, where the outcome genuinely is identical.

**A suspended account is redirected to `/billing`** (`AppLayout`). It could previously reach every screen and do almost nothing on them — the first action it tried failed with nothing pointing at billing as the cause. `SUSPENDED_ALLOWED_PATHS` keeps `/billing` (the only screen that resolves it) and `/settings` (read-mostly, spends nothing, and a seller choosing a plan may want to see what is configured) reachable. Gated on `enforcementEnabled`, so with enforcement off nothing redirects.

### Quota top-ups (migration `087`)

**Only the tracking-conversion meter is sold as a top-up, and that follows from the three measures being different kinds of thing.** Listings are a LEVEL — "more listings for this month" is not a coherent product, wanting more is a plan upgrade. The automatic-order ceiling is an ANTI-ABUSE guard on the Playwright pool, and selling past it means selling the one resource money cannot buy more of. Conversions are the priced meter with a real linear unit cost, so selling more is a straightforward margin sale. Adding a second product line later is a row in `billing_quota_addons`, never a new table — but it needs the same argument made first.

- **Credits are scoped to ONE UTC calendar month** and raise the ceiling; they are never decremented. `resolveEffectiveLimit` = plan limit + `SUM(credits for this month)`. A credit that outlived the month would need its own consumption ledger — a second accounting of the same quantity, and those drift. The obvious objection (buy on the 28th, lose it on the 1st) is answered in the product, not the schema: **packs are offered ONLY to a seller who has actually reached the limit**, so nobody is sold an allowance they cannot use, and the offer appearing is itself the signal that they are constrained.
- **`resolveEffectiveLimit` lives in the REPOSITORY, and that placement is load-bearing.** Both the quota gate and the billing summary call it. If the gate resolved the plan limit while the summary added credits, a seller who had just topped up would see headroom and still be refused — the worst possible outcome for something they just paid for. A credit lookup that fails falls back to the PLAN limit, never to unlimited: costing someone headroom they paid for is visible and fixable, handing out unmetered allowance silently is not.
- **This is the ONE `checkout.session.completed` we act on.** A one-time purchase creates no Subscription, so that event is the only signal it ever produces — and it carries only what we put in `metadata` (`addon_slug`) and `client_reference_id` (the user id). Subscription checkouts still ignore the event (the customer is linked before checkout opens); the handler returns early unless `mode === 'payment'`. It also requires `payment_status === 'paid'`, because Checkout can complete while an asynchronous method is still settling and granting on the promise gives quota away free.
- **Granting is idempotent on the Stripe event id** (`billing_quota_credits.provider_event_id UNIQUE`). The webhook inbox already dedupes; free allowance is not something to protect with one layer.
- Packs need `pnpm --filter api stripe:sync-catalog` to mint their Stripe **one-time** prices (`prices.create` WITHOUT `recurring` — a recurring price is refused by `mode: 'payment'`). Until then `isPurchasable` is false and the FE shows the pack disabled rather than letting a seller click into a 409.
- Seeded packs: 100 / 250 / 500 conversions at $19.99 / $44.99 / $79.99 — all comfortably profitable after Stripe fees against the ~$0.10 unit cost (~46% / ~41% / ~34%).

### Admin billing metrics

`GET /v1/admin/billing/metrics` is a read-only, role-gated endpoint displayed in the admin Billing tab. It reports cost totals, account/access distributions, and listing/AO quota pressure. Unknown costs remain `null`; real zero costs remain `0`. It uses the existing `UserRole.ADMIN`, `RolesGuard`, and privileged-session guards — do not introduce a second RBAC system.

### Two operator CLIs (no HTTP path, like `user:set-role`)

Both load `apps/api/.env` for `DATABASE_URL` and construct only the repository classes they need — no NestJS bootstrap, so no BullMQ processor, scheduler or boot-time migration run starts as a side effect. Exit codes follow `user-set-role.ts` (`0` done/dry-run, `2` bad args, `3` no such user, `1` refused or DB failure).

- **`pnpm --filter api reset-users`** (`src/scripts/reset-users.ts`) wipes user accounts from a NON-production database so the signup → trial → onboarding flow can be re-run from scratch. **Dry run by default** — it prints the row counts it would destroy and exits; `--yes` is required to actually delete, `--email <e>` scopes to one user, `--products` also clears the shared ASIN cache. It **refuses when `NODE_ENV=production`** and refuses a `DATABASE_URL` that is not on this machine unless `--allow-remote` is passed; it prints the target host/database first, and runs as one transaction. The delete plan is a frozen list in `reset-users-helpers.ts`, asserted against a protected-table list so catalog/config data (plans, templates, `platform_settings`, learned eBay taxonomy) can never be caught up in it. It issues an **explicit DELETE against `ebay_trial_ledger`, `billing_customers` and `aquiline_profiles`** because each is deliberately not FK-cascaded from `users` — the trial ledger must survive an account deletion to stop trial farming (migration `084`), and the Aquiline profile is permanent provider-side so its local row is decoupled from the account cascade.
- **`pnpm --filter api billing:reconcile -- --email a@b.c`** (`src/scripts/billing-reconcile.ts`, `--dry-run` to preview) re-reads a customer's live subscription straight from Stripe and re-applies it to `billing_subscriptions` through the **same** `BillingRepositoryService.upsertSubscriptionByProvider` + `extractStripeSubscriptionFields` the webhook applier uses — never its own SQL, because two writers of that row is the drift this module has repeatedly suffered. It exists because the read-time UNPAID→SUSPENDED rule above means webhook silence can suspend an account, so the recovery path must not itself depend on a webhook. It does **not** re-enqueue anything — auto-fulfill resumes on its own via the lazy sweep in `OrderSyncService.syncOrdersForAccount` on the next order-sync tick once the account is entitled again — it only reports that.

## Order Management

### Data Flow
```
eBay Order → Order Sync (BullMQ, every 20 min) → orders table
  ↕ (matched via lineItem.legacyItemId → listings.ebay_item_id)
orders.listing_id → listings → products (title, image, ASIN via JOIN)
  ↓ (NEW order only, xmax-detected insert)
Sale-Driven Stock Sync → products.stock decremented → stock-sync queue → recompute + push eBay
  ↓ (automatic, every 30 min per Amazon account)
Amazon Order Auto Cost-Capture (amazon-order-sync queue) → scrape account order list
  → strict-match ASIN+qty+amount+date to pending/provisional eBay orders → write real costs → LINKED
  ↓ (or user manually links an Amazon Order ID)
Amazon Account + Amazon Order ID → Playwright scraping → order costs updated → recomputeProfit
  ↓ (automatic, when store_settings.auto_fulfill_enabled + per-account enable+cap; A2)
Automated Amazon Fulfillment (`auto-fulfill` queue) → Playwright checkout on round-robin buyer account
  → review-step hard cap → place order → atomic write real costs + LINKED + schedule tracking
  ↓ (automatic)
Amazon Order Tracking (BullMQ, per-order scheduler) → Amazon status polling → eBay status sync
```

### Order → Listing → Product Relationship
- **Matching**: eBay order's `lineItems[0].legacyItemId` matched against `listings.ebay_item_id` during sync
- **Product data** (title, ASIN, image, SKU): Resolved via `orders.listing_id → listings → products` JOIN in `findOne()`
- **Purchase price**: Stored in orders as snapshot (product prices change, order needs historical price)
- **Order table** only stores: eBay order data, buyer info, financials, shipping address
- **Columns**: `order_date` (eBay creationDate), `last_ebay_event_at` (eBay lastModifiedDate), `updated_at` (our updates), `last_synced_at` (sync tracking)

### Net Profit Formula
```
netProfit = ebayEarnings - purchasePrice - amazonTax - amazonShipping         (confirmed/linked)
netProfit = ebayEarnings - purchasePrice - (purchasePrice × amazonTaxRate/100) (provisional estimate)
```
- `ebayEarnings` = eBay's `totalDueSeller` (already deducts all eBay fees)
- `transactionFee` and `adFee` are calculated and stored for display only, NOT deducted again
- **`net_profit` is NULLABLE** (migration `033`): `NULL` = unknown cost (never faked); `0` = a computed real zero (trusted Amazon link with costs that legitimately sum to zero, e.g. free shipping + no tax). Aggregations `FILTER` out NULL rows rather than treating them as 0.
- **`cost_capture_status` enum** (`packages/shared` `OrderCostCaptureStatus`, DB type `order_cost_capture_status`) drives profit confidence. Values:
  - `pending` — eBay order ingested, nothing captured yet, product cost unknown.
  - `linked` — Amazon costs fully scraped from a real Amazon order. **TRUSTED**: only status that produces a headline `net_profit`.
  - `provisional` — listing matched so product/purchase cost is known, but Amazon tax+shipping not yet captured (awaiting auto cost-capture or manual link).
  - `failed` — Amazon scrape ran but returned no usable financials; prior values retained, `net_profit` stays NULL.
  - `untracked` — no listing match (`listing_id IS NULL`); source cost can never be resolved. Stays NULL forever.
- **Provisional estimate (A1.1)**: when `cost_capture_status = provisional`, `recomputeProfit` computes an **estimate** via the pure `estimateProvisionalNetProfit({ ebayEarnings, purchasePrice, amazonTaxRatePct })` helper in `profit-calculation.ts`. Uses the product's last-known Amazon price (resolved via `orders.listing_id → listings → products`); Amazon shipping is **not** estimated (variable; often $0 on Prime — disclosed in UI). The tax percent comes from the per-user global `amazonTaxRate` store setting (migration `035`, default `0`). On any store-settings failure, `recomputeProfit` falls back to `0%` (gross) — settings resolution never breaks profit compute.
- **`profitBasis`** (`'confirmed' | 'estimated' | null`) on `OrderDto` + dashboard rows: derived from `cost_capture_status` by `deriveProfitBasis` (`linked→'confirmed'`, `provisional→'estimated'`, else `null`). The FE uses it to label estimates; the **confirmed (linked) dashboard headline stays pure/trusted** — provisional rows are surfaced separately, never blended into the headline total.
- **Single writer**: `OrderSyncService.recomputeProfit(ebayOrderId, { scrapeFailed? })` is the only method that sets `cost_capture_status`. Called on order insert, on `ebay_earnings` change during re-sync, and after every Amazon link / cost-capture path. Renamed from `recalculateProfit` (old name still appears in git history only). Resolves the user's global store settings best-effort via `StoreSettingsService.getResolvedSettings(userId, null)` when computing the provisional estimate.
- **Dashboard profit tiers** (`GET /dashboard`, `dashboard.service.ts buildPeriod`):
  - `confirmed` = `SUM(net_profit) FILTER (status <> cancelled AND cost_capture_status = 'linked')` — the **headline** `netProfit` shown on period cards. Trusted only; `profitBasis = 'confirmed'`.
  - `provisional` = same filter with `cost_capture_status = 'provisional'` — shown separately as a lower-confidence addendum, labeled "Estimated" in the FE (`profitBasis = 'estimated'`). Never blended into the headline total.
  - `uncosted` = `SUM(sale_total) FILTER (cost_capture_status IN ('pending','failed','untracked'))` — **revenue only**, no profit implied (`profitBasis = null`).
- **ASIN availability for untracked fallback (open question — resolved)**: eBay's inbound order payload does **not** expose the item ASIN; the listing-match path uses `lineItems[].legacyItemId` → `listings.ebay_item_id`. The `recomputeProfit` ASIN fallback resolves via `orders.listing_id → listings → products.asin` JOIN, which is only available when a listing matched. For truly untracked orders (no listing) the ASIN is **not resolvable** from the eBay payload — those rows correctly stay `untracked` + `NULL net_profit`. The auto cost-capture matcher's ASIN signal comes from the same JOIN on the eBay candidate side (Amazon side ASIN comes from the scrape).

### Order Sync (eBay → Local)
- **Queue**: `order-sync` (BullMQ), cron every 20 min (`*/20 * * * *`, raised from 15 min on 2026-08-12 to trim shared Fulfillment quota spend — see the budget note below)
- **Per-user processing**: Each user gets a separate job, concurrency: 3
- **Manual refresh**: `POST /orders/sync` queues high-priority job, waits, returns fresh data. **It has no seller-facing entry point (removed 2026-08-11)** — eBay meters the Fulfillment API per APPLICATION, and the 20-minute cron alone already costs `72 × accounts` calls/day against a 100,000/day ceiling (~36% at 500 accounts). A user-spammable pull spends that shared quota for almost no new information, and exhausting it stops order sync for *every* seller. The endpoint stays for operator/debug use; do not wire a Refresh button to it.
- **Budget-governed (2026-08-12)**: `EbayFulfillmentService.fetchOrders` acquires `EbayApiResource.FULFILLMENT` from `EbayCallBudgetService` before every call (`EbayBudgetExhaustedError` → the processor defers the affected user's sync to the next tick rather than throwing raw into BullMQ retry). Previously this was the single biggest real consumer of the Fulfillment ceiling and the only one of the five `EbayApiResource` values with **zero** call sites acquiring it — the admin "eBay Limits" tab showed 0/limit for Fulfillment while it was actually the resource running closest to its ceiling. The shipped-tracking push (`createShippingFulfillment`) still bypasses the governor — same gap, not yet closed.
- **Cache tracking**: `ebay_accounts.last_ebay_sync_at` — sync fetches only orders since last sync
- **Listing matching**: Uses `lineItem.legacyItemId` → `listings.ebay_item_id` to establish `listing_id`
- **Idempotency**: `upsertOrder()` uses Postgres `RETURNING id, (xmax = 0) AS inserted` to detect brand-new orders vs re-synced updates — one-time side effects (stock decrement) only fire on genuine inserts
- **Tracking-at-ingest invariant**: every eBay-account order is ingested and visible, but `listing_id` is assigned only when the Item ID matches a SellerHill listing that is ACTIVE at first ingest. Sale-driven stock sync and auto-fulfill require that initial match. `ON CONFLICT` deliberately never backfills `listing_id`, so importing the listing later cannot retroactively auto-purchase an older untracked order. `order-tracking-invariant.guard.spec.ts` locks all three gates.
- **Re-sync cost recompute**: when an existing order's `ebay_earnings` changes on re-sync (delta > $0.001), `OrderSyncService.recomputeProfit(ebayOrderId)` is re-invoked so `net_profit` + fees reflect the corrected earnings. `cost_capture_status` is preserved/advanced, never reset to `pending`.
- **`listings.sold_count` is incremented in real time (2026-08-11)** — same genuine-insert gate as sale-driven stock sync (`inserted && listingId && quantity > 0`): `UPDATE listings SET sold_count = sold_count + quantity`, in its own try/catch so a failure here can never block stock sync or auto-fulfill. It used to be written once at listing creation/import and never touched again, so it went stale immediately. `listings.watch_count`/`view_count` were removed from the entire stack (DTOs, API, list/detail UI, filters, CSV export, i18n) for the same reason discovered at the same time — nothing ever wrote a real value to them (eBay's watcher/view counts require the Trading API `GetItem` call, which this platform deliberately does not use — see "eBay API call budget"), so they always rendered 0. The DB columns (`watch_count`, `view_count`) were **dropped by migration `095`** (2026-09-06) — `010`/`017` (which created them) are untouched, the drop is a new file.

### Sale-Driven Stock Sync (between Keepa refresh cycles)
- **Why**: A confirmed eBay sale is real signal that Amazon stock dropped, so we don't wait for the next Keepa refresh cycle to correct eBay quantities.
- **Queue**: `stock-sync` (BullMQ). Producer = `StockSyncQueueService` (orders module, `@InjectQueue`), consumer = `StockSyncProcessorService` (listings module, `@Processor`, concurrency 3). Same queue name registered in both modules → same Redis queue.
- **Flow on new matched order**: `products.stock` decremented by order quantity (`ProductsService.decrementStock`, floors at 0) → enqueue `{ productId }` → worker calls `ProductSyncService.syncListingsForProduct(productId)` → reuses the same fan-out as the refresh pipeline: recomputes `quantity` per listing's own settings group and pushes to eBay (`updatePriceAndStock`) + DB.
- **Quantity formula** (single source of truth, `ListingStrategyService.calculateQuantity`): `quantity = min(max(amazonStock − buffer, 0), defaultQuantity)`. Used by listing creation, the Keepa refresh pipeline, and the sale-driven queue identically.
- **Shared product stock**: `products.stock` is an ASIN-level cache shared across all customers. One customer's sale depletes it, so ALL listings sharing that ASIN are recomputed — each with its own group's `buffer`/`defaultQuantity` → different eBay quantities per seller.
- **Best-effort & idempotent**: stock sync is wrapped so it never fails order sync; the worker re-reads `products.stock` at execution time, so delayed/coalesced jobs re-push the correct current value. The Keepa refresh pipeline resets `products.stock` to Amazon ground truth.
- **Rate-limit hygiene**: BullMQ `jobId` bucketed per 5s window per product collapses a burst of sales into one job; `attempts: 3` + exponential backoff; `EbayService.withRateLimitRetry` honours `Retry-After` on 429/5xx.

### Amazon Order Linking (manual)
- User provides Amazon Order ID + selects an Amazon Account
- `AmazonScrapingService` scrapes the Amazon order detail page via Playwright
- Scraped data (purchase price, tax, shipping, tracking) written to order, `amazon_linked_at` set
- If the scrape reached the page but the financial DOM was empty, the caller preserves prior costs and invokes `OrderSyncService.recomputeProfit(ebayOrderId, { scrapeFailed: true })` to mark `cost_capture_status = failed` (never silently zero-fills)
- On a successful link, `recomputeProfit(ebayOrderId)` runs with the real costs already on the row → sets `cost_capture_status = linked` and computes a trusted `net_profit`
- Tracking job started immediately for the order

### Amazon Order Auto Cost-Capture (`amazon-order-sync` queue)
Automatically links Amazon costs to pending/provisional eBay orders without manual order-ID entry. One BullMQ job per Amazon buyer account scrapes that account's "Your Orders" list, then strict-matches each scraped Amazon order to an eBay candidate and writes real costs.

- **Queue**: `amazon-order-sync` (BullMQ, `AMAZON_ORDER_SYNC_QUEUE`). Cron `AMAZON_ORDER_SYNC_CRON` (default `0 */3 * * *` — every 3 hours), one job per Amazon account. Concurrency: `AMAZON_ORDER_SYNC_CONCURRENCY` (default 2).
- **This tick is the platform's biggest scaling lever, and its cadence is a capacity decision, not a latency one (2026-08-10).** It costs one Playwright scrape per **account** per fire whether or not that account sold anything, so its cost is `accounts × ticks/day` and is **independent of order volume**. `AmazonRateLimiter` caps the whole platform at 5 concurrent browser actions ⇒ ~432k browser-seconds/day; at 500 accounts × ~35s the former every-30-minutes default demanded ~840k/day — roughly **2× the available supply**, i.e. a permanent backlog that never drains. Every 3 hours costs ~140k/day and fits. Raising the frequency only shortens how soon **already-placed** orders get their Amazon costs written: purchasing is triggered directly from order ingest (`maybeEnqueueAutoFulfill`) and tracking has its own per-order schedulers, so nothing a seller waits on runs here. Tune it at `/admin` → Settings (`amazon.orderSync.cron`, `requiresRestart`) before sizing up hardware.
- **Per-account flow** (`AmazonOrderSyncService.runForAccount`):
  1. Select candidate eBay orders for the user with `cost_capture_status IN ('pending','provisional')` and `order_date >= NOW() - 60 days` (ASIN resolved via `orders → listings → products` JOIN).
  2. `AmazonScrapingService.scrapeAccountOrders(userId, accountId, since)` — Playwright scrape of the account's order list since `amazon_accounts.last_orders_sync_at` (first sync defaults to 30-day look-back). Returns `{ rows, suspect }`.
  3. For each Amazon order, `pickBestMatch` (pure helper) picks the highest-scoring eBay candidate via `scoreAmazonOrderMatch` (**strict**: requires ALL of same ASIN + same quantity + amount within tolerance + date within window; any miss → no match, never force-link).
  4. On a confident match: write Amazon `purchase_price`/`amazon_tax`/`amazon_shipping`, set `amazon_linked_at`, set `cost_capture_status = linked`, call `recomputeProfit(ebayOrderId)` → trusted `net_profit`.
  5. Misses stay `pending`/`provisional` for the next tick or manual linking.
- **Watermark discipline** (Keepa-aligned, no silent drops):
  - **Transport failure** (Playwright crash, Amazon 5xx, network): `scrapeAccountOrders` throws → BullMQ retries the whole job with exponential backoff; `last_orders_sync_at` is NOT advanced so the same window is re-pulled.
  - **Suspect 0-row scrape** (page redirected off "Your Orders", or zero order-cards on page 1 — likely a layout/selector break): treated as a data failure, NOT a legit empty. Watermark is held (no advance) and the run returns without throwing — the 30-min scheduler naturally retries on the next tick, avoiding a BullMQ retry storm on a persistent Amazon layout change.
  - **Legit empty / no candidates**: watermark advances normally.
  - **Per-Amazon-order failure** in the match/write loop: logged and skipped, never fails the run (watermark still advances for the rest).
- **Matching strictness** (`order-matcher.ts`): `scoreAmazonOrderMatch` requires identical ASIN (`+40`), identical quantity (`+20`), amount within `AMAZON_ORDER_SYNC_MATCH_TOLERANCE_PCT` percent (`+up to 40`, scaled by closeness), date within `AMAZON_ORDER_SYNC_MATCH_WINDOW_DAYS` (`+up to 20`, scaled by closeness). Score is informational; `match: true` requires ALL four gates to pass. Wrong cost attribution is treated as worse than no attribution — there is no "best effort" force-link path.
- **Config (all optional, defaults shown):**
  - `AMAZON_ORDER_SYNC_CRON='0 */3 * * *'` — scheduler tick (every 3 hours; see the capacity note above before lowering it).
  - `AMAZON_ORDER_SYNC_CONCURRENCY=2` — parallel jobs (one per Amazon account).
  - `AMAZON_ORDER_SYNC_MATCH_TOLERANCE_PCT=5` — max percent diff between Amazon `grandTotal` and eBay `sale_total`.
  - `AMAZON_ORDER_SYNC_MATCH_WINDOW_DAYS=7` — max day-delta between Amazon and eBay order dates (candidate pool itself is 60-day to cover slow ship paths).

### Amazon Order Tracking (Amazon → eBay Status Sync)
- **Queue**: `amazon-tracking` (BullMQ, per-order schedulers)
- **Intervals** (env-tunable, hours): pre-ship `AMAZON_TRACKING_PRESHIP_INTERVAL_HOURS` (default **6** — the only urgency is pushing the tracking number to eBay soon after Amazon ships); shipped `AMAZON_TRACKING_SHIPPED_INTERVAL_HOURS` (default **24** — delivered-detection has no time-critical side effect, and 12h polling of the longest order phase doubled scrape traffic for nothing)
- **No auto-stop**: Tracking continues until order reaches a terminal state (delivered, cancelled, completed). The processor removes the job when terminal status is detected.
- **eBay sync**:
  - Amazon `shipped` → `POST /sell/fulfillment/v1/order/{id}/shipping_fulfillment` on eBay with tracking number
  - Amazon `delivered` → Order status set to `completed`; if the order was never observed `shipped` (fast delivery between ticks), the eBay fulfillment push runs first so eBay always gets the tracking
- **Hardened semantics (2026-07-28, `amazon-tracking-processor.service.ts`)**:
  - **Fresh eBay token**: the shipped push uses `EbayService.getAccountAccessToken(ebayAccountId)` (refresh-on-demand) — never the raw `ebay_accounts.access_token` column, which is expired by the time the 6–12h tick fires.
  - **No silent drop**: a failed eBay fulfillment POST propagates out of `handleShipped` → the local status write is skipped → the next tick retries the push. Only permanently-unpushable conditions (account gone/inactive, no linked listing item) log-and-advance.
  - **Fresh tracking on transition**: the freshly scraped tracking number/carrier are mirrored into the in-memory row before the shipped push (the row was read pre-scrape and is empty on the first shipped detection).
  - **Status regression guard** (`shouldApplyStatus`): no-op writes, transitions out of terminal states, and SHIPPED → pre-ship downgrades are blocked (the Amazon parser's `'pending'` fallback on a layout miss must not rewind an order and cause a duplicate eBay fulfillment later).
  - **Interval downshift**: on the shipped transition the per-order scheduler is re-upserted at the 12h interval (previously only a restart's `reconcileSchedulers()` applied it).
  - **Amazon-cancel ≠ eBay-cancel**: an Amazon-side cancellation (migration `056`) stamps `orders.amazon_cancelled_at` and stops tracking but NEVER overwrites the local eBay order status — the eBay sale is still live and must be fulfilled another way. Such orders surface in the "needs attention" filter (`auto_fulfill_status IN (blocked,failed) OR amazon_cancelled_at IS NOT NULL`) and as an "Amazon cancelled" badge in the orders list.
  - **Tracking kickoff on auto-link**: `AmazonOrderSyncService` schedules per-order tracking immediately after a confident cost-capture match (previously tracking only started at the next API restart via `reconcileSchedulers`).
- **On startup**: `reconcileSchedulers()` cleans up orphaned schedulers and creates missing ones for active orders

### Automated Amazon Fulfillment (A2)

When `store_settings.auto_fulfill_enabled` is on, a brand-new matched eBay order automatically triggers an Amazon purchase on a round-robin buyer account. Fires from the **same `upsertOrder` `xmax = 0` genuine-insert seam** as sale-driven stock-sync — A2 only runs on the first ingest of an order, never on re-syncs. Intended for the dropshipping flow: eBay sale → buy on Amazon → ship-to buyer → Amazon tracking forwarded to eBay.

- **Queue**: `auto-fulfill` (BullMQ, literal `AUTO_FULFILL_QUEUE = 'auto-fulfill'`, registered in `OrdersModule` via `BullModule.registerQueue`). Producer = `AutoFulfillQueueService` (orders module) invoked from `OrderSyncService.maybeEnqueueAutoFulfill` on the genuine-insert path. Processor = `AutoFulfillProcessor extends WorkerHost` (amazon module). `jobId = fulfill-${ebayOrderId}` dedup, `attempts: 3`, exponential backoff 60s. Concurrency: `AUTO_FULFILL_QUEUE_CONCURRENCY` (default 1) — sequential per the rate-limiter contract.
- **Producer resolution** (`OrderSyncService.maybeEnqueueAutoFulfill`, pure helpers in `auto-fulfill-helpers.ts`):
  1. `StoreSettingsService.getResolvedSettings(userId, entity.ebayAccountId)` (store-specific override falls back to the user's global row — same Store > Global > Default resolution as the rest of `store_settings`; fixed 2026-07-28, previously always passed `null` and silently ignored any per-store `autoFulfillEnabled` override) → if `!autoFulfillEnabled` → status `skipped`, no enqueue.
  2. `SELECT id, last_used_at, auto_fulfill_cap_total FROM amazon_accounts WHERE user_id=$1 AND auto_fulfill_enabled=TRUE AND auto_fulfill_cap_total IS NOT NULL` → empty → `skipped`.
  3. `pickRoundRobinAccount` picks oldest-`last_used_at`-first (NULL treated as oldest); ties broken by id ASC.
  4. `meetsCoarseCapGate(saleTotal, cap)` pre-filter — `saleTotal > 0 && saleTotal <= cap`. Obvious over-cap orders never enqueue.
  5. `UPDATE amazon_accounts SET last_used_at = NOW` on the picked account → `enqueue(ebayOrderId, amazonAccountId)`.
- **Checkout service** (`AmazonCheckoutService`, step-structured Playwright). Every browser action goes through `AmazonRateLimiter.schedule(accountId, …)`. Step sequence inside `runForOrder → checkout`:
  1. **Idempotency re-check** — re-reads `orders.auto_fulfill_status`; `shouldSkipFulfillStart` (PLACED/BLOCKED/DRY_RUN/SKIPPED) → log + return. Prevents double-order on BullMQ retries.
  2. `loadInputs` (joins amazon_accounts + listings + products) — throws `no_asin` if no row, `cap` on non-finite cap.
  3. `ensureLoggedIn` via `AmazonScrapingService.ensureAuthenticatedPage` → maps captcha/OTP/login signals to `captcha`/`otp`/`login`.
  3b. **Cart hygiene** — `clearCart` best-effort empties the active cart BEFORE adding our item (a blocked prior attempt or the buyer's personal items would otherwise be co-purchased — Amazon checks out the WHOLE cart).
  4. Add-to-cart with quantity — `out_of_stock` if ASIN unavailable or no visible add-to-cart button.
  4b. **Cart verification (HARD, fail-closed)** — `verifyCartContents` navigates to the cart page and requires EXACTLY one active line item matching our ASIN (and, when readable, the order quantity) → otherwise throws `cart` + `cart-mismatch` evidence snap. An unreadable row count also blocks; selector drift surfaces during dry-run tuning, before money moves.
  5. `selectShipToAddress` (throws `address`), `selectDefaultPayment` (throws `payment` on decline signals).
  **Ship-to must be the eBay BUYER, never the buyer account's default.** `loadInputs` refuses the order (`address`) when `orders.shipping_address` lacks street/city/zip — it previously defaulted to `{}`, and the flow then "used whatever Amazon pre-selected", i.e. the account holder's own address: a paid order delivered to the wrong person with the eBay sale still unfulfilled. Saved-address selection uses the pure, unit-tested `addressBlockMatchesBuyer` (`address-match.ts`), which requires street **and** zip (plus the unit line when the buyer has one) — a zip-only rule silently picked the wrong entry when one household held two addresses under the same zip (observed live: `30 N GOULD ST 24233` vs `STE567`). Name is corroborating only, since eBay and Amazon render recipients differently. No match → `addBuyerAddress` enters the buyer's address; if that is unavailable the order fails closed.
  6. **Review-step HARD CAP** — reads grand total on the review page (the last step before "Place Order"). The **Place Order control must be visible** before the total is accepted (`review_unreadable` otherwise): Amazon's address step also renders an "Order total" sidebar, so without this gate the cap was checked against an earlier step's figure and dry-run reported success without ever proving the final pre-purchase page. The total is read from labelled selectors first, then a structure-independent `/order total|grand total\s*:?\s*\$N/` text fallback anchored to the LABEL (never "first price on the page", which could be a subtotal). Non-finite/≤0 → `review_unreadable` (distinct from `cap`, so a selector break is never misreported as the spend guard working); `> capTotal` when `AUTO_FULFILL_REVIEW_CAP_HARD_STOP !== 'false'` (default ON) → `cap`. Aborts BEFORE the click, never over-spends.
  7. **Dry-run** (`amazon_accounts.auto_fulfill_dry_run`) → snap `dry_run_review`, then `simulatePlacement` + return. NO click.
  **Dry-run simulates the post-purchase bookkeeping** so an operator can verify what a real placement does to an order — which costs land, that it becomes cost-captured, what net profit is computed — without money leaving. It writes a `SIM-`-prefixed `amazon_order_id` (`SIMULATED_AMAZON_ORDER_PREFIX`), splits the REAL review-page total into `purchase_price`/`amazon_tax`/`amazon_shipping`, sets `cost_capture_status='linked'`, and calls the real `recomputeProfit` so `net_profit` is produced exactly as production would. Three safety properties are load-bearing: `auto_fulfill_status` stays **DRY_RUN** (never `placed`, so `shouldSkipFulfillStart` and the PURCHASED state are untouched); the `SIM-` prefix makes `deriveFulfillmentState` report SIMULATED; and **tracking is NOT scheduled** — the id is fake, so a tracker would scrape a non-existent Amazon order and mark the row failed. Fail-soft throughout: a diagnostic aid must never break the run it inspects.
  8. Else click "Place Order" (click errors swallowed at warn-log to prevent double-order races), `parseConfirmation` (throws `no_confirmation` on missing confirmation DOM), `onPlaced`, set status `placed`.
- **Money safety — `onPlaced` (fail-soft layered)**: after a confirmed placement, NO path rethrows into the processor's failure branch.
  - **Layer 1** (single atomic UPDATE): `auto_fulfill_status='placed'` + `cost_capture_status='linked'` + real `purchase_price`/`amazon_tax`/`amazon_shipping` + `amazon_order_id` + `amazon_linked_at=NOW` in one statement.
  - **Layer 2** (fallback if layer 1 throws): minimal UPDATE setting `amazon_order_id` + `placed` only.
  - **Layer 3** (best-effort try/catch): `OrderSyncService.recomputeProfit(ebayOrderId)` → trusted `net_profit` (A1 reuse — single writer invariant preserved).
  - **Layer 4** (best-effort try/catch): `AmazonTrackingQueueService.scheduleOrderTracking(orderId, amazonAccountId)` → existing tracker drives shipped→eBay shipped, delivered→completed.
- **Fail-closed typed errors**: `AutoFulfillBlockedError` carries one of the shared blocked reasons (`no_asin`, `captcha`, `otp`, `login`, `out_of_stock`, `address`, `payment`, `cap`, `no_confirmation`, `cart`). `AmazonCheckoutService.runForOrder` catches its OWN blocked errors → sets `auto_fulfill_status='blocked'` + `auto_fulfill_blocked_reason` + returns (NO BullMQ retry — blocked is a permanent data/state condition, not transport). Any error that ESCAPES to `AutoFulfillProcessor.process` is transport/infra: on the final attempt it sets `auto_fulfill_status='failed'` then rethrows so BullMQ applies backoff. The idempotency re-check inside `runForOrder` is what keeps retries safe across the producer→processor boundary.
- **Evidence screenshots**: full-page PNGs at `fulfillment-evidence/{ebayOrderId}/{stage}-{timestamp}.png` via `snap(page, ebayOrderId, stage)` (path overridable via `FULFILLMENT_EVIDENCE_DIR`, default `$CWD/fulfillment-evidence`). Stages include each blocked-reason, `dry_run_review`, and the order confirmation. **No admin-role gating is enforced in code** — the directory is treated as admin-only via filesystem perms on the server.
- **Guardrails (defense-in-depth)**: master toggle (store settings) + per-account enable/cap/dry-run (amazon_accounts) + **cap-required-to-enable** (server-side `AmazonAccountsService.assertCanEnable` throws `amazon.errors.autoFulfillCapRequired` if `capTotal == null` when the flag is on) + review-step hard cap + coarse pre-filter + dry-run + fail-closed typed errors + idempotency re-check. A proxy is deliberately **not** part of this gate — see "Amazon Scraping — Anti-Ban Strategy" below.
- **Config (all optional, defaults shown):**
  - `AUTO_FULFILL_QUEUE_CONCURRENCY=1` — parallel checkout jobs (keep at 1 to honour per-account rate limiting).
  - `AUTO_FULFILL_CHECKOUT_MIN_TIME_MS=4500` — minimum spacing between checkout steps (human-like pacing).
  - `AUTO_FULFILL_REVIEW_CAP_HARD_STOP=true` — any value ≠ literal `'false'` keeps the review-step cap on (default ON).
  - `FULFILLMENT_EVIDENCE_DIR=$CWD/fulfillment-evidence` — screenshot output dir.
  - `FULFILLMENT_EVIDENCE_TTL_DAYS=7` — evidence PNG retention; `AmazonCheckoutService` sweeps per-order dirs older than this hourly (default 7d, clamped 1–365).
- **Settings UI — two gates, both customer-facing.** Auto-fulfillment only runs when BOTH are on, matching `maybeEnqueueAutoFulfill`'s resolution order:
  1. **Store gate** — `autoFulfillEnabled` toggle in the live `StoreSettingsDrawer`, set globally or per eBay store (Store > Global > Default, same as `amazonTaxRate`). The drawer also carries the tracking-conversion provider Select (only `local` visible; `api` labeled "coming soon").
  2. **Buyer-account gate** — `autoFulfillEnabled` toggle + `autoFulfillCapTotal` in the live `AmazonAccountDrawer`, per Amazon buyer account. A user with two buyer accounts must opt each one in separately. Enabling is refused by `assertCanEnable` without a cap; the drawer surfaces the backend's own error key (`autoFulfillCapRequired`) rather than a generic failure. Proxy is a separate, optional section of the same drawer — see below.

  **`auto_fulfill_dry_run` is deliberately NOT in any customer UI.** It exists to validate the Playwright `CHECKOUT_SELECTORS` against live Amazon DOM — a platform engineering concern with no customer meaning. Operators set it directly on `amazon_accounts` for the one-time selector-tuning pass. (The former `features/amazon/accounts/AmazonAccountsPage` carried all three fields but was orphaned — no route, no importer — when settings moved to the hub+drawer model; it was deleted and its customer-facing fields moved into `AmazonAccountDrawer`. **Credential verify is now reachable** as a per-card "Verify login" action in `AmazonAccountsDrawer` — re-saving the password purely to trigger a retry was not a usable recovery path, since verification can fail for reasons unrelated to the stored credentials (stale session, proxy hiccup, Amazon challenge). Account delete is still unreachable in the UI — a known gap.)
